<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="t6fn1">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline">ASH, ash content; TPC, total phenolic
content estimation; FRAP, FRAP analysis; DPPH, DPPH analysis; ABTS,
ABTS analysis; <em>L</em>*, brightness; <em>a</em>*, redness coordinate; <em>b</em>*, yellowness coordinate;
TPC, total phenolic content; and LC–MS, metabolomic analysis.
Bold values mean the most significant correlations.</p>
</div></div></section><p>Biplot PCA was also built to explore and correlate
the antioxidant
capacity, total phenolic content estimation, ash content, colorimetry,
and metabolomics analyses (<a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">9</a></a>). <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">9</a></a>A shows the PCA biplot from flours, in which PC1 explained
67% of the variance and was related to the three antioxidant assays,
metabolomics analysis, ash content, redness, and yellowness coordinates.
From this model, PC1 separates sorghum and pearl millet flours from
the other flours. Similarly, whole sorghum and pearl millet flours
were separated from the other wholegrain flours (<a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">9</a></a>B). The sum of PC1 and PC2
was 81%, and PC1 (64%) was associated with antioxidant analyses, metabolomics
analysis, total phenolic content, and redness coordinate. PC2 was
responsible for 18% of the variance and separation of whole barley,
triticale, and rye flours from whole oats and wheat flours except
for W1. The PCA biplot from bran samples showed PC1 (61%) associated
with antioxidant analyses, metabolomics analysis, redness, and yellowness
coordinates (<a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">9</a></a>C). From this analysis, colored pericarp sorghum (S1–S3) and
pearl millet brans were separated by PC1 from the other bran samples.
PC2 (22%) separates barley and rye brans from triticale and wheat
brans. <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig9" class="usa-link">9</a></a>D displays
the PCA biplot from the husk samples, where the sum of PC1 and PC2
was 76%. Oat husks were clearly separated from the barley husks by
PC1 (50%), in which this component was associated with metabolomics
analysis, total phenolic content, antioxidant capacity, redness, and
yellowness coordinates.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig9"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/f6ca/11363145/dcf22bdfeb6a/jf4c01312_0009.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr9" height="2851" width="4175" alt="Figure 9"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Principal component analyses (PCA) biplot of
physicochemical, antioxidant
activity, phenolic content, and metabolomics analyses of flours (A),
wholegrain flours (B), brans (C), and husk fractions (D). Dot: dependent
variables (results); diamond: samples. ASH, ash content; <em>L</em>*, brightness; <em>a</em>*, redness coordinate; <em>b</em>*, yellowness coordinate; TPC, total phenolic content; and LC–MS,
total relative abundance of free and bound extracts provided by the
metabolomics analysis.</p></figcaption></figure><p>This work seems to be the first in characterizing
and comparing
through high-resolution metabolomic approaches major and minor cereal
crops, concerning both different milling fractions and the number
and diversity of species, which differ in the botanical taxonomy.
Analyzing all of these different samples under the same conditions
(<em>e.g.</em>, using the same metabolite extraction procedure,
metabolomics workflow, and antioxidant assays) allows us a more confident
comparison among samples, since they were treated equally. Additionally,
this work contributes to providing metabolomics data of pearl millet
and, especially, of triticale grains, which still shows a lack of
information when analyzed by metabolomics approaches.</p>
<p>The influence
of tannins in the antioxidant capacity, total phenolic
content, and distinct metabolite profiling was noticeable, drawing
attention to outstanding tannin-rich sorghum genotypes. Sorghum and
millet appeared as distinct crops, and even those with no pigmented
testa and tannin-free also exhibited great results. The milling processing
also affects the qualitative and quantitative composition of the cereal
fractions, in addition to the metabolite composition. Refined flours
showed the lowest antioxidant capacity, phenolic content, ash content,
and relative abundance of specialized metabolites. Remarkably, byproducts
of cereal milling (husk and bran) showed the greatest antioxidant
potential, phenolic content, and relative abundance of metabolites
compared to the flours and wholegrain flours. In this way, this work
shows the bioactive potential of byproducts and adds evidence to further
studies.</p></section></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>
<p>The authors thank Prof. Dr. Luiz Carlos Gutkoski,
Dr. Cristina
Takeiti, Dr. Carlos Wanderlei Piler, Dr. Valeria Queiroz, EMBRAPA
Milho e Sorgo, EMBRAPA Trigo, Universidade de Passo Fundo, OR Melhoramento
de Sementes, and Biotrigo Genética for providing and collecting
samples; to Dr. Michael Murgu from Waters Corporation (Brazil) for
his helpful technical assistance; and to Prof. Norberto Peporine and
Dr. Luis Guilherme Feitosa from NPPNS (FCFRP-USP) for essential teachings
in GNPS.</p></section><section id="dl1" class="glossary"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Glossary</h2>
<h3>Abbreviations and Nomenclature</h3>
<dl class="def-list">
<dt>AACCI</dt>
<dd><p>American Association of Cereal
Chemists International</p></dd>
<dt>ABTS</dt>
<dd><p>2,2′-azino-bis(3-ethylbeenzothiazoline-6-sulfonic acid)
diammonium salt</p></dd>
<dt>ANOVA</dt>
<dd><p>analysis of variance</p></dd>
<dt>BPC</dt>
<dd><p>bound compounds</p></dd>
<dt>CE</dt>
<dd><p>catechin equivalents</p></dd>
<dt>DW</dt>
<dd><p>dry weight</p></dd>
<dt>FPC</dt>
<dd><p>free compounds</p></dd>
<dt>FRAP</dt>
<dd><p>ferric-reducing antioxidant power</p></dd>
<dt>GAE</dt>
<dd><p>gallic acid equivalents</p></dd>
<dt>HCA</dt>
<dd><p>hierarchical cluster analysis</p></dd>
<dt>PCA</dt>
<dd><p>principal component
analysis</p></dd>
<dt>PLS-DA</dt>
<dd><p>partial least-squares-discriminant analysis</p></dd>
<dt>QC</dt>
<dd><p>quality control</p></dd>
<dt>TPC</dt>
<dd><p>total phenolic content</p></dd>
<dt>Trolox</dt>
<dd><p>6-hydroxy 2,5,7,8-tetramethylchroman-2-carboxylic
acid</p></dd>
<dt>TE</dt>
<dd><p>trolox equivalents</p></dd>
<dt>UHLPC–HRMS</dt>
<dd><p>ultrahigh
performance liquid chromatography coupled to high-resolution mass
spectrometry</p></dd>
<dt>VIP</dt>
<dd><p>variable importance for the projection</p></dd>
